Intrinsic Wasserstein Rates for Score-Based Generative Models on Smooth Manifolds
Guoji Fu, Taiji Suzuki, Wee Sun Lee, Atsushi Nitanda

Abstract
Score-based generative models are trained in high-dimensional ambient spaces, yet many data distributions are supported on low-dimensional nonlinear structures. We prove that, for compact d d -dimensional smooth manifolds ℳ ⊂ [ 0 , 1 ] D \mathcal{M}\subset[0,1]^{D} with d > 2 d>2 and β \beta -Hölder densities strictly positive on ℳ \mathcal{M} , a variance-preserving SGM estimator attains the intrinsic Wasserstein–1 sample exponent 𝒪 ~ ( D 𝒪 β ​ ( d ) n − ( β + 1 ) / ( d + 2 β ) ) \tilde{\mathcal{O}}(D^{\mathcal{O}_{\beta}(d)}n^{-(\beta+1)/(d+2\beta)}) , up to logarithmic factors and explicit geometry and density factors. The full nonasymptotic bound explicitly isolates the finite-order geometry envelope, Hölder radius, density lower bound, ambient dependence, and finite-order correction terms. The analysis separates score approximation into a large-noise tangent-cell regime and a small-noise projection-centered, de-Gaussianized Laplace regime. The key technical ingredient is a ReLU implementation of nearest-projection coordinates via finite intrinsic anchors and Gauss–Newton iterations, rather than approximating the manifold projection as a black-box high-dimensional smooth map. Consequently, for families with polynomially controlled geometry and density lower bounds, the constructed score-network parameters have polynomial ambient dependence.



1
Introduction

Score-based Generative Models (SGMs), also known as diffusion models
(
SWMG 15
;
SE 19
;
SSK+ 21
;
HJA 20
;
VKK 21
)
, are foundational to high-dimensional generative modeling across domains such as imagery
(
DN 21
)
, video
(
HJA 20
)
, audio
(
CZZ+ 21
;
KPH+ 21
)
, and biological structures
(
WJB+ 23
)
. However, their statistical behavior remains insufficiently understood when the ambient dimension
D
is large but the data exhibit an intrinsic dimension
d\ll D
. The manifold hypothesis
(
FMN 16
)
postulates that the relevant sample complexity should scale with
d
, independent of the ambient nonparametric dimension.

This paper provides a rigorous quantitative foundation for the manifold hypothesis within the context of diffusion models.
Assuming the data distribution is supported on a compact
d
-dimensional smooth manifold
{\mathcal{M}}\subset[0,1]^{D}
and admits a
\beta
-Hölder density, bounded below with respect to the volume measure on
{\mathcal{M}}
, we prove a nonasymptotic
{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
guarantee for a variance-preserving SGM with explicit ambient, density, and finite-order geometric dependence. In the fixed-geometry regime with
d>2
and the canonical finite-order choice, the bound attains the intrinsic sample exponent
n^{-(\beta+1)/(d+2\beta)}
up to logarithmic and displayed polynomial ambient factors.

A primary theoretical challenge arises because manifold-supported distributions are singular with respect to the Lebesgue measure in
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
. At time zero, the ambient score is undefined. At any positive time, Gaussian smoothing induces a score characterized by normal localization toward the manifold tube and tangential transport along the manifold surface. These distinct phenomena require different stable approximation strategies. The large-noise regime interacts with tangent patches and can be analyzed using intrinsic cell counts
\sigma_{t}^{-d}
. Conversely, the small-noise regime necessitates a projection-centered ratio expansion because the score’s denominator contains a singular normal Gaussian factor. This dichotomy motivates the two-regime analytical framework developed throughout this paper.

While ambient-space SGM theory offers critical insights, it does not directly resolve this question. Convergence analyses have evolved from requiring Log-Sobolev assumptions
(
LLT 22
;
WY 22
)
to accommodating weaker moment or Fisher-information conditions
(
BBDD 24
;
CDS 23
)
. Concurrently, score-estimation theories now yield sharp ambient rates for Besov and Hölder densities
(
OAS 23
;
DKXZ 24
;
SAL 25
)
, with recent advances even relaxing the need for density lower bounds
(
ZYLL 24
;
FL 25
)
. Because these results are tailored to full-dimensional densities in
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, they do not by themselves explain why the distribution-learning rate should depend on
d
for singular laws on nonlinear manifolds.

Other complementary perspectives study manifold detection and score singularities
(
Pid 22
;
LWB 23
;
LSHH 25
)
or broad
{\mathsf{W}}_{p}
generalization through Wasserstein dimension
(
CBB 26
)
. These address objectives different from smooth-density distribution learning, and therefore do not yield the dimension-explicit intrinsic
{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
rate targeted here.

has the intrinsic sample exponent, but the dimension-explicit constants depend on how the projection step is instantiated.
\ddagger

and the two small-noise regime conditions; the table entry records the
d>2
simplification after that term is absorbed.

Comparison with prior manifold analyses.

Table

1
highlights prevailing theoretical gaps.
Linear-support models recover intrinsic rates but bypass curvature challenges
(
OAS 23
;
CHZW 23
)
.
For nonlinear manifolds, earlier analyses either incur exponential-in-
D
prefactors
(
TY 24
)
or derive slower rates with high-dimensional approximation factors
(
YP 25
)
.
A recent support-aware analysis obtains a sharper displayed ambient prefactor, roughly
\sqrt{D}

(
ADR 24
)
, but it does so for a more structured pipeline: the estimator first builds sample-dependent local low-rank models of the support, its main score-learning target is weighted by
\sigma_{t}^{2}
, and the final rate carries an
n^{\delta}
slack.
Our theorem targets a different constructive problem: it certifies an explicit ambient score-network realization of the projection-centered small-noise score, without preprocessing the manifold into local affine subproblems, and attains the exact intrinsic sample exponent up to logarithms.
See
Section

A.2
for the theorem-level comparison.

Comparison with the concurrent work
(
ZHZ+ 26
)
.

The dagger marks their displayed theorem rate in
Table

1
.
They obtain the same intrinsic sample exponent as our result, and the distinction is not the exponent but the dimension-explicit construction behind it.
Their proof invokes a generic ambient smooth-map approximation step for the nearest projection on the reach tube. Under a standard
D
-variate implementation, that step entails a high-order ambient approximation audit for
\Pi_{{\mathcal{M}}}
.
In contrast, our proof adopts a finite-anchor Gauss–Newton network in intrinsic coordinates, yielding a constructive polynomial ambient audit of the score network under the stated polynomial-geometry regime. See
Section

A.1
for the detailed comparison.

1.1
Our contributions

In the displayed bounds below,
\Gamma_{{\mathcal{M}},q_{\rm geom}}
denotes the finite-order geometry envelope defined in
Definition

B.5
. It records reach, volume, and finite-order atlas smoothness factors explicitly; these factors are not hidden by
\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(\cdot)
.
The primary contributions are:

(i)

Intrinsic Wasserstein estimation under explicit geometric assumptions:

Under
1
,
2
and
3
, the polynomial control
D\vee p_{\min}^{-1}\leq n^{a_{0}}
, and the explicit small-noise reach and denominator-resolution conditions, we prove

{\mathbb{E}}\,{\mathsf{W}}_{1}(P_{0},\widehat{P}_{t_{0}}^{\rm lb})\leq\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}\!\Bigl(\Gamma_{{\mathcal{M}},q_{\rm geom}}^{C}B_{0}^{C}p_{\min}^{-C}(D\vee\log n)^{\mathfrak{p}_{\beta,d}}\bigl[\mathfrak{r}_{n}+n^{-1/2}+n^{-1/2}\mathfrak{r}_{n}^{-d/(2q_{\star})}\bigr]\Bigr),

where
\mathfrak{r}_{n}=n^{-(\beta+1)/(d+2\beta)}
. For
d>2
and the canonical finite-order choice
q_{\rm geom}=q_{\rm opt}(\beta)
, the finite-order cap term is
{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{r}_{n})
, yielding the intrinsic exponent in the fixed-geometry sense.

(ii)

Explicit ambient dependence and admissible growth regime:

The constructed score networks have leading width and sparsity
n^{d/(d+2\beta)}
and an explicit ambient factor
(D\vee\log n)^{\mathfrak{p}_{\beta,d}}
, where
\mathfrak{p}_{\beta,d}
is displayed in
Corollary

H.1
and satisfies
\mathfrak{p}_{\beta,d}={\mathcal{O}}_{\beta}(d)
after the
d>2
finite-order choice. For fixed geometry and fixed density lower bound,
Corollary

H.2
records a sufficient ambient-growth condition

D\vee\log n\leq c\,n^{a_{\beta,d}}
under which the two small-noise checks hold.
For growing-ambient families, the polynomial-in-
D
interpretation additionally requires polynomial control of the finite-order geometry envelope and density factors.
Section

B.7
verifies this geometry regime for affine spheres, flat product tori, and finite-order/reach-controlled closed graph-type embeddings.

(iii)

Constructive two-regime score approximation:

The large-noise branch uses tangent-cell approximation with intrinsic cell counts, while the small-noise branch uses projection-centered de-Gaussianized Laplace expansions, a finite-anchor Gauss–Newton network for the projection center, affine chart-coordinate extraction, and a polynomial denominator lower bound before reciprocal-network evaluation. Both branches are verified on the forward tube and then passed through clipped score-estimation and slabwise
{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
perturbation arguments.

Technical overview.

The proof separates the Gaussian footprint into two geometric regimes. At large noise, the smoothed score can be approximated by tangent-cell expansions with intrinsic cell counts, so the construction never needs an ambient grid. At small noise, the proof recenters at the nearest point on
{\mathcal{M}}
, factors out the common normal Gaussian term, and approximates only the tangential Laplace coefficients and the de-Gaussianized ratio. The dimension-explicit step is a finite-anchor Gauss–Newton ReLU network for the projection center, followed by affine chart-coordinate extraction; this replaces a black-box high-order
D
-variate approximation of
\Pi_{{\mathcal{M}}}
. The detailed two-regime proof architecture is given in
Section

3
, with appendix audit maps in
Sections

B.2
and
B.3
.

2
Background

2.1
Notation

Let
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\coloneqq\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}\mid x\geq 0\}
denote the space of non-negative real numbers,
{\mathbb{N}}\coloneqq\{0,1,2,\dots\}
the set of natural numbers, and
{\mathbb{N}}_{+}\coloneqq{\mathbb{N}}\setminus\{0\}
.
We let
{\mathcal{N}}({\bm{0}},\sigma^{2}{\bm{I}}_{D})
represent the Gaussian distribution on
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
with mean vector
{\bm{0}}
and covariance matrix
\sigma^{2}{\bm{I}}_{D}
.
For real numbers
a
and
b
, we write
a\vee b\coloneqq\max\{a,b\}
and
a\wedge b\coloneqq\min\{a,b\}
.
We use standard asymptotic notation:
a={\mathcal{O}}(b)
implies
a\leq Cb
for a universal constant
C>0
, while
a={\mathcal{O}}_{\beta}(b)
indicates
a\leq C_{\beta}b
, where the constant
C_{\beta}
may depend on the fixed smoothness parameter
\beta
but is independent of
d,D,n,\sigma_{t},p_{\min}
, and any numerical geometry constants. The notation
\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(\cdot)
hides logarithmic factors, and
\lesssim
suppresses constants independent of the displayed variables in the surrounding statement.
Unadorned constants
C,c>0
are universal and may change from line to line; named or subscripted constants may depend only on the quantities explicitly stated in the surrounding lemma, theorem, or convention.
For any
\beta>0
,
\lfloor\beta\rfloor
denotes the largest integer strictly less than
\beta
(equivalently,
\lfloor\beta\rfloor=\lceil\beta\rceil-1
).
For a multi-index
{\bm{\alpha}}=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{k}
and a vector
{\bm{v}}=(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
, we define
{\bm{v}}^{{\bm{\alpha}}}\coloneqq v_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}v_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}\cdots v_{k}^{\alpha_{k}}
and
|{\bm{\alpha}}|\coloneqq\alpha_{1}+\dots+\alpha_{k}
.

The main theorems use a finite geometry order chosen once and then held fixed:

q_{\rm geom}\in{\mathbb{N}},\qquad q_{\rm geom}\geq q_{\min}(\beta):=\left\lceil\max\{\beta+4,2(\beta\vee 1)+2\}\right\rceil.

(1)

This order controls the finite Taylor expansions used in the tangent-cell proof.
For the
d>2
optimal
{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
theorem we additionally choose it so that

q_{\rm geom}-2\geq d(\beta+1)/(d-2)
. Since
d
is an integer and
d>2

implies
d/(d-2)\leq 3
, this can be enforced uniformly by taking the canonical
choice

q_{\rm geom}=q_{\rm opt}(\beta):=\left\lceil\max\{q_{\min}(\beta),2+3(\beta+1)\}\right\rceil.

(2)

Equivalently, any fixed order bounded by a constant depending only on
\beta

and satisfying the same lower bounds gives the same ambient exponent.
Thus the finite order is
{\mathcal{O}}_{\beta}(1)
and is never allowed to grow with
n
,
D
, or
d
.
In the appendix, our bookkeeping collects direct ambient powers into an exponent
\mathfrak{p}_{\beta,d}
after this finite order has been fixed.
Under the preceding
d>2
choice, the explicit bookkeeping gives
\mathfrak{p}_{\beta,d}={\mathcal{O}}_{\beta}(d)
.
Within the main text, these rates are summarized as
(D\vee\log n)^{{\mathcal{O}}_{\beta}(d)}
or, after hiding logarithms, as
D^{{\mathcal{O}}_{\beta}(d)}
.
We also fix the target
{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
sample rate and the large-noise accuracy-cap exponent:

\mathfrak{r}_{n}:=n^{-(\beta+1)/(d+2\beta)},\qquad q_{\star}:=q_{\rm geom}-2.

(3)

The symbol
\mathfrak{r}_{n}
is reserved for this final sample rate.
Uppercase
R
-symbols are used only for local radii, cutoffs, or chartwise integral labels.
Additional proof-bookkeeping conventions restricted to the appendix are collected in
Section

B.5
.

Wasserstein distance.

For
P,Q
two probability distributions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, their Wasserstein-
1
distance is

{\mathsf{W}}_{1}(P,Q)\coloneqq\inf_{\gamma\in\Gamma(P,Q)}\int\|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{y}}\|_{2}{\mathrm{d}}\gamma({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}),

and
\Gamma(P,Q)
is the set of all couplings of
P
and
Q
.
We use
{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
as it remains meaningful for singular or disjoint manifold-supported measures and respects the ambient geometry of the sampling space.

Neural network class.

Following standard neural network approximation theory
(
Yar 17
;
SH 20
)
, we model the score function using deep ReLU neural networks with explicit constraints on depth, width, parameter sparsity, and magnitude.
Specifically, we define the score network
s({\bm{x}},t)
as a function mapping the concatenated input
({\bm{x}},t)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
to the score vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
.

Definition 2.1

(Sparse ReLU neural network)
.

Let
\rho(u)=\max\{u,0\}
act coordinatewise.
For a width vector
{\bm{W}}=(W_{0},W_{1},\dots,W_{L})
, the class
{\mathcal{NN}}(L,{\bm{W}},S,B)
consists of all maps
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{W_{0}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{W_{L}}
obtained from the recursion
{\bm{z}}_{0}={\bm{z}},{\bm{z}}_{\ell}=\rho\!\bigl({\bm{A}}^{(\ell)}{\bm{z}}_{\ell-1}+{\bm{b}}^{(\ell)}\bigr),1\leq\ell\leq L-1,f({\bm{z}})={\bm{A}}^{(L)}{\bm{z}}_{L-1}+{\bm{b}}^{(L)}
, where
{\bm{A}}^{(\ell)}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{W_{\ell}\times W_{\ell-1}}
and

{\bm{b}}^{(\ell)}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{W_{\ell}}
for
\ell=1,\dots,L
, and
\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\bigl(\|{\bm{A}}^{(\ell)}\|_{0}+\|{\bm{b}}^{(\ell)}\|_{0}\bigr)\leq S,\max_{1\leq\ell\leq L}\bigl(\|{\bm{A}}^{(\ell)}\|_{\infty}\vee\|{\bm{b}}^{(\ell)}\|_{\infty}\bigr)\leq B
.
For score networks, we take
W_{0}=D+1
and
W_{L}=D
.

2.2
Score-based Generative Models (SGMs)

Forward process.

As a forward process
({\bm{X}}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, we consider the Variance Preserving (VP) process:

{\mathrm{d}}{\bm{X}}_{t}=-\alpha(t){\bm{X}}_{t}{\mathrm{d}t}+\sqrt{2\alpha(t)}{\mathrm{d}}{\bm{B}}_{t},\quad(0\leq t\leq T)\quad{\bm{X}}_{0}\sim P_{0},

(4)

where
({\bm{B}}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
denotes an independent
D
-dimensional standard Brownian motion and we have that
{\bm{X}}_{t}|{\bm{X}}_{0}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(m_{t}{\bm{X}}_{0},\sigma_{t}^{2}{\bm{I}}_{D})
, where
m_{t}=\exp(-\int_{0}^{t}\alpha(\tau){\mathrm{d}}\tau),\sigma_{t}^{2}=1-\exp(-2\int_{0}^{t}\alpha(\tau){\mathrm{d}}\tau)
.

Reverse process.

The VP process
Equation

4
has a reverse process
({\bm{Y}}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
, where
{\bm{Y}}_{t}={\bm{X}}_{T-t}
satisfies

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}{\bm{Y}}_{t}\!=\!\alpha(T\!-\!t)({\bm{Y}}_{t}+\!2\nabla\log p_{T-t}({\bm{Y}}_{t})){\mathrm{d}t}\!+\!\sqrt{2\alpha(T\!-\!t)}{\mathrm{d}}{\bm{B}}_{t}^{\prime},\quad(0\leq t\leq T)\qquad{\bm{Y}}_{0}\sim P_{T},

where
({\bm{B}}_{t}^{\prime})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is another independent
D
-dimensional standard Brownian motion and
\nabla\log p_{t}(\cdot)
is called the score function.
However, the noise distribution
P_{T}
and score function
\nabla\log p_{t}(\cdot)
are unknown.
It has been shown that the VP process converges exponentially to the standard Gaussian distribution
{\mathcal{N}}({\bm{0}},{\bm{I}}_{D})

(
CCL+ 23
)
and for sufficiently large
T
, we can replace
P_{T}
by
{\mathcal{N}}({\bm{0}},{\bm{I}}_{D})
.

Score matching loss.

To estimate the true score function
s^{*}({\bm{x}},t)\coloneqq\nabla\log p_{t}({\bm{x}})
, we learn a score estimator
s:{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\times[t_{0},T]\to{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
through minimizing the score matching loss:

{\mathcal{L}}_{[t_{0},T]}(s)\coloneqq\int_{t_{0}}^{T}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bm{X}}_{t}}[\|s({\bm{X}}_{t},t)-\nabla\log p_{t}({\bm{X}}_{t})\|_{2}^{2}]{\mathrm{d}t}.

(5)

Because this exact objective is computationally inaccessible, we optimize the equivalent
denoising score matching
loss
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bm{X}}_{0}}[\ell(s,{\bm{X}}_{0})]

(
Hyv 05
;
Vin 11
)
, where:

\displaystyle\ell(s,{\bm{X}}_{0})\!\coloneqq\!\!\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\!\!{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bm{X}}_{t}|{\bm{X}}_{0}}[\|s({\bm{X}}_{t},t)\!-\!\nabla\log p_{t}({\bm{X}}_{t}|{\bm{X}}_{0})\|_{2}^{2}]{\mathrm{d}t}.\!

It is well established that
{\mathcal{L}}_{[t_{0},T]}(s)-{\mathcal{L}}_{[t_{0},T]}(s^{*})={\mathbb{E}}_{{\bm{X}}_{0}}[\ell(s,{\bm{X}}_{0})-\ell(s^{*},{\bm{X}}_{0})]
.

Empirical risk minimization (ERM).

Given
n
i.i.d training samples
{\bm{x}}^{(1)},\dots,{\bm{x}}^{(n)}\sim P_{0}
, we learn the score estimator
\widehat{s}
over the hypothesis class
{\mathcal{NN}}
via the ERM:

\widehat{s}\in\argmin_{s\in{\mathcal{NN}}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(s,{\bm{x}}^{(i)}).

(6)

Early stopping.

As
t\to 0
, the underlying score function
\nabla\log p_{t}(\cdot)
diverges, causing the reverse SDE to become highly ill-conditioned.
To ensure numerical stability, we truncate the reverse dynamics, terminating the simulation at a small positive time threshold
t_{0}>0
.

Generating new samples.

Using the trained score estimator
\widehat{s}
across the interval
[t_{0},T]
, we construct an approximate reverse process
(\widehat{{\bm{Y}}}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T-t_{0}}
and draw new samples
{\bm{X}}^{\prime}\coloneqq\widehat{{\bm{Y}}}_{T-t_{0}}\sim\widehat{P}_{t_{0}}
via:

{\mathrm{d}}\widehat{{\bm{Y}}}_{t}\!=\!\alpha(T\!-\!t)(\widehat{{\bm{Y}}}_{t}+2\widehat{s}(\widehat{{\bm{Y}}}_{t},T\!-\!t)){\mathrm{d}t}+\!\sqrt{2\alpha(T\!-\!t)}{\mathrm{d}}{\bm{B}}_{t},\quad(0\leq t\leq T-t_{0})\qquad\widehat{{\bm{Y}}}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}({\bm{0}},{\bm{I}}_{D}).

2.3
Manifold Hypothesis

Assumption 1

(Compact smooth manifold)
.

The predictor
{\bm{X}}_{0}\sim P_{0}
is supported on
{\mathcal{M}}\subset[0,1]^{D}
, where
{\mathcal{M}}
is a compact,
C^{\infty}
,
d
-dimensional Riemannian manifold without boundary isometrically embedded in
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, with
1\leq d\ll D
and reach
\kappa>0
and Riemannian volume
S_{{\mathcal{M}}}:=\mathrm{vol}_{{\mathcal{M}}}({\mathcal{M}})
.

Throughout the approximation bounds below,
d
is treated as the intrinsic dimension and its dependence is kept separate from the ambient-dimension dependence on
D
.

The reach controls curvature and self-avoidance of the normal tube, while
S_{{\mathcal{M}}}
controls finite chart counts.
Their precise contribution to constants is recorded in the finite-order geometry envelope in
Definition

B.5
.
Concrete families satisfying the resulting polynomial-geometry regime, including affine spheres, flat product tori, and closed graph-type embeddings with finite-order derivative and reach control, are given in
Section

B.7
.

Definition 2.2

(Hölder functions on a Riemannian manifold)
.

Fix a finite
C^{\lceil\beta\rceil}
atlas
\{(U_{i},\phi_{i})\}_{i\in{\mathcal{A}}}
on the compact manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
, with coordinate domains
\phi_{i}(U_{i})\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, and use this atlas in the definition below.
For
\beta>0,B_{0}\geq 1
, a function
f:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
belongs to
{\mathcal{H}}^{\beta}({\mathcal{M}},B_{0})
if, for every chart
(U_{i},\phi_{i})
in this fixed atlas, with
f^{(i)}:=f\circ\phi_{i}^{-1}:\phi_{i}(U_{i})\to{\mathbb{R}}
, the following hold:

(i)

All
\partial^{{\bm{\alpha}}}f^{(i)}
with
\|{\bm{\alpha}}\|_{1}\leq\lfloor\beta\rfloor
exist continuously and
\max_{\|{\bm{\alpha}}\|_{1}\leq\lfloor\beta\rfloor}\|\partial^{{\bm{\alpha}}}f^{(i)}\|_{L^{\infty}(\phi_{i}(U_{i}))}\leq B_{0}
.

(ii)

For every
\|{\bm{\alpha}}\|_{1}=\lfloor\beta\rfloor
,

\sup_{{\bm{u}}\neq{\bm{v}}\in\phi_{i}(U_{i})}\frac{|\partial^{{\bm{\alpha}}}f^{(i)}({\bm{u}})-\partial^{{\bm{\alpha}}}f^{(i)}({\bm{v}})|}{\|{\bm{u}}-{\bm{v}}\|_{2}^{\beta-\lfloor\beta\rfloor}}\leq B_{0}.

The radius
B_{0}
is therefore a controlled chartwise radius, not a supremum over arbitrary smooth reparametrizations.
Passing from this atlas to the reach-scale projection atlas used in the proof only changes the radius by finite transition-map constants; see
Lemma

D.4
.

Assumption 2

(Hölder smooth density on manifold)
.

The law
P_{0}
of
{\bm{X}}_{0}
is absolutely continuous w.r.t the Riemannian volume measure
\mathrm{vol}_{{\mathcal{M}}}
on
{\mathcal{M}}
.
We write
{\mathrm{d}}P_{0}({\bm{y}})=p_{0}({\bm{y}}){\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{vol}_{{\mathcal{M}}}({\bm{y}})
, where
p_{0}:{\mathcal{M}}\to[0,\infty)
satisfies
p_{0}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\beta}({\mathcal{M}},B_{0})
w.r.t the fixed controlled finite atlas for a radius
B_{0}\geq 1
and
\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}p_{0}({\bm{y}}){\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{vol}_{{\mathcal{M}}}({\bm{y}})=1
.

Assumption 3

(Positive density lower bound)
.

There exists a constant
p_{\min}\in(0,1]
such that
p_{0}({\bm{y}})\geq p_{\min},\forall{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{M}}
.

The appendix recalls the standard chart, atlas, and partition-of-unity conventions and constructs the finite projection atlas, reach-volume tube bound, and finite-order geometry envelope
\mathcal{K}_{{\mathcal{M}},q}
used to bound approximation constants; see
Sections

B.4
,
B.6
and
D.3
.
These are bookkeeping devices for localization and network-size controls, whereas the fundamental statistical assumptions governing the main theorem are stated explicitly here.

3
Score Approximation on Smooth Manifolds

This section states the deterministic comparison networks later inserted into the clipped ERM oracle and the
{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
perturbation argument.
Recall
s^{*}({\bm{x}},t)=\nabla\log p_{t}({\bm{x}})
and
{\bm{X}}_{t}=m_{t}{\bm{X}}_{0}+\sigma_{t}{\bm{Z}}
, with
{\bm{X}}_{0}
supported on
{\mathcal{M}}\subset[0,1]^{D}
.
The small-noise class has leading intrinsic capacity
n^{d/(d+2\beta)}
, while each large-noise slab has capacity
\sigma_{t_{k-1}}^{-d}+r_{\star,n}^{-d}
.
The cap is kept explicit below and, under the final
d>2
choice of
q_{\star}
, is no larger than the assembled leading capacity up to displayed geometry and logarithmic factors.
No statistical estimation is performed in this section.

3.1
Tube Localization

For any ambient coordinate
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, define the Euclidean distance to the rescaled manifold as:

\mathrm{dist}({\bm{x}},m_{t}{\mathcal{M}})\coloneqq\inf_{{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{M}}}\|{\bm{x}}-m_{t}{\bm{y}}\|_{2}.

For any fixed off-tube precision exponent
A>0
, choose
C_{\varrho}=C_{\varrho}(A)>0
sufficiently large and define the active tube parameters:

\varrho_{t}\coloneqq C_{\varrho}\sigma_{t}\sqrt{D\vee\log n},\qquad A_{t}\coloneqq\{{\bm{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}:\mathrm{dist}({\bm{x}},m_{t}{\mathcal{M}})\leq\varrho_{t}\}.

(7)

The radius matches the Gaussian footprint up to a logarithmic tail margin, so the core approximation problem is localized to
A_{t}
; outside
A_{t}
, clipping and Gaussian concentration suffice.

Lemma 3.1

(Informal version of
Lemma

C.3
)
.

Under
1
, fix any
A>0
and choose the tube constant
C_{\varrho}=C_{\varrho}(A)
sufficiently large.
If an arbitrary comparison score
\widetilde{s}
satisfies the uniform clipping bound
\sup_{{\bm{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}\|\widetilde{s}({\bm{x}},t)\|_{\infty}\leq C\sigma_{t}^{-1}(D\vee\log n)^{1/2}
, then the off-tube risk is bounded by:

\int_{A_{t}^{c}}\|\widetilde{s}({\bm{x}},t)-s^{*}({\bm{x}},t)\|_{2}^{2}p_{t}({\bm{x}}){\mathrm{d}}{\bm{x}}\leq C_{A}\sigma_{t}^{-2}(D\vee\log n)^{C}n^{-A},

where the constant
C_{A}
may depend on the prescribed off-tube exponent
A
.

3.2
Near-Tube Integral Representation

On
A_{t}
, a projection atlas and partition of unity reduce the smoothed manifold density to chartwise Gaussian integrals; the full construction is in
Section

D.3
.
Throughout this section we use that finite projection atlas, denoted
\{(U_{i},\phi_{i})\}_{i=1}^{C_{{\mathcal{M}}}}
, where
\phi_{i}:U_{i}\to\phi_{i}(U_{i})\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is the affine tangent-projection chart, together with a subordinate partition of unity
\{\rho_{i}\}_{i=1}^{C_{{\mathcal{M}}}}
with compact supports
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}\Subset U_{i}
.
We write
{\bm{z}}_{i}:=\phi_{i}^{-1}
on
\phi_{i}(U_{i})
,
{\bm{J}}_{i}({\bm{u}}):=\nabla{\bm{z}}_{i}({\bm{u}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D\times d}
, and
{\bm{G}}_{i}({\bm{u}}):={\bm{J}}_{i}({\bm{u}})^{\top}{\bm{J}}_{i}({\bm{u}})
.
Let
{\mathcal{Q}}({\bm{x}},t)
be the Gaussian-weighted manifold density and
{\mathcal{P}}({\bm{x}},t)
its first moment.
Aggregating them isolates the centered numerator:

{\mathcal{U}}({\bm{x}},t)\coloneqq\frac{m_{t}{\mathcal{P}}({\bm{x}},t)-{\bm{x}}{\mathcal{Q}}({\bm{x}},t)}{\sigma_{t}}.

This directly yields the ambient density and target score representation:

p_{t}({\bm{x}})=(2\pi\sigma_{t}^{2})^{-D/2}\sigma_{t}^{d}{\mathcal{Q}}({\bm{x}},t),\qquad\sigma_{t}s^{*}({\bm{x}},t)=\frac{{\mathcal{U}}({\bm{x}},t)}{{\mathcal{Q}}({\bm{x}},t)}.

Thus, the stable approximation target is the scaled, centered score
\sigma_{t}s^{*}({\bm{x}},t)
, avoiding a separate approximation of
m_{t}{\mathcal{P}}
and
{\bm{x}}{\mathcal{Q}}
followed by cancellation and division by
\sigma_{t}
.

3.3
Proof Architecture for Score Approximation

The score approximation problem is dictated by the geometry of the Gaussian
footprint around the scaled manifold
m_{t}{\mathcal{M}}
.
On the active tube
A_{t}
, the scaled score has the centered-ratio representation
\sigma_{t}s^{*}({\bm{x}},t)={\mathcal{U}}({\bm{x}},t)/{\mathcal{Q}}({\bm{x}},t)
displayed above.
The two approximation mechanisms are:

Regime

Geometry mapped by Gaussian

Primary obstruction

Approximation mechanism

Large noise regime
[t_{1},T]

Tangent-scale manifold patch

Avoiding an ambient spatial grid while integrating over the forward tube

Tangent-cell approximation at radius
c_{0}\sigma_{t_{k-1}}\wedge r_{\star,n}

Small noise regime
[t_{0},t_{1}]

Normal fiber intersecting the nearest projection point

A
\sigma_{t}
-mesh is statistically prohibitive, and the raw denominator contains an infinitesimal normal Gaussian factor

Projection-centered, de-Gaussianized Laplace expansion

Thus, large noise is handled by intrinsic tangent cells, while small noise is handled by centering at
\Pi_{{\mathcal{M}}}({\bm{x}}/m_{t})
, factoring out the common normal Gaussian, and expanding only in
d
tangential coordinates.
The former keeps the cell count at
\sigma_{t_{k-1}}^{-d}+r_{\star,n}^{-d}
; the latter avoids the prohibitive
\sigma_{t_{0}}^{-d}
mesh and avoids treating
\Pi_{{\mathcal{M}}}
as a generic
D
-variate smooth function.
The key dimension-explicit ingredient in the small-noise branch is the projection network: it computes the center
\Pi_{{\mathcal{M}}}({\bm{x}}/m_{t})
by finite intrinsic anchors and Gauss–Newton iterations, and obtains chart coordinates by affine projection charts. In contrast, the large-noise branch uses affine tangent-plane linearization centers for local integration, not nearest projection.

3.4
Large Noise: Tangent-Cell Approximation

On a large-noise slab
I_{k}=[t_{k-1},t_{k}]\subset[t_{1},T]
, partition each chart box into coordinate cells of side length

h_{k}=c_{0}\sigma_{t_{k-1}}\wedge r_{\star,n}
, with
r_{\star,n}
defined in

Proposition

G.2
.
The resulting complexity is the intrinsic cell count
\sigma_{t_{k-1}}^{-d}+r_{\star,n}^{-d}
rather than an ambient grid. For a cell center
{\bm{u}}_{\nu}
, set
{\bm{y}}_{\nu}={\bm{z}}_{i}({\bm{u}}_{\nu})
and define the affine tangent-plane center

\mathsf{T}_{\nu}({\bm{x}},t)\coloneqq m_{t}{\bm{y}}_{\nu}+\mathsf{P}_{\nu}^{\rm tan}({\bm{x}}-m_{t}{\bm{y}}_{\nu}).

For
{\bm{y}}={\bm{z}}_{i}({\bm{u}})
in the cell,

\|{\bm{x}}-m_{t}{\bm{y}}\|_{2}^{2}=\|{\bm{x}}-\mathsf{T}_{\nu}({\bm{x}},t)\|_{2}^{2}+2\langle{\bm{x}}-\mathsf{T}_{\nu}({\bm{x}},t),\mathsf{T}_{\nu}({\bm{x}},t)-m_{t}{\bm{y}}\rangle+\|\mathsf{T}_{\nu}({\bm{x}},t)-m_{t}{\bm{y}}\|_{2}^{2}

decouples the phase into a tangent Gaussian envelope plus higher-order curvature corrections. The denominator satisfies the tube floor

{\mathcal{Q}}({\bm{x}},t)\geq C^{-1}\Gamma_{{\mathcal{M}},q_{\rm geom}}^{-C}p_{\min}\exp\{-C\Gamma_{{\mathcal{M}},q_{\rm geom}}^{C}(D\vee\log n)\},\qquad{\bm{x}}\in A_{t}.

The network approximates these cellwise envelopes and their stored chart moments, then constructs the reciprocal on the displayed denominator range.

Proposition 3.2

(Informal version of
Theorem

E.5
)
.

Under
1
,
2
and
3
and the polynomial-growth regime
D\vee p_{\min}^{-1}\leq n^{a_{0}}
, consider the dyadic large-noise grid with capped cell radius

h_{k}=c_{0}\sigma_{t_{k-1}}\wedge r_{\star,n}
.
The cap
r_{\star,n}
is chosen in
Proposition

G.2
precisely so that the phase-smallness and fixed-order geometry conditions in
Equation

65
hold on every large-noise slab.
Then, for every
I_{k}
, there exists a clipped ReLU network
s_{k}^{\rm lg}\in{\mathcal{NN}}(L_{k},{\bm{W}}_{k},S_{k},B_{k})
such that, for all
{\bm{x}}\in A_{t}
and
t\in I_{k}
:

\|s_{k}^{\rm lg}({\bm{x}},t)-s^{*}({\bm{x}},t)\|_{2}^{2}\leq C\Gamma_{{\mathcal{M}},q_{\rm geom}}^{C}B_{0}^{C}p_{\min}^{-C}\sigma_{t}^{-2}n^{-2(\beta+1)/(d+2\beta)}(D\vee\log n)^{\mathfrak{p}_{\beta,d}}.

Moreover,
\sup_{{\bm{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}\|s_{k}^{\rm lg}({\bm{x}},t)\|_{\infty}\leq C\sigma_{t}^{-1}(D\vee\log n)^{1/2}
, with capacity bounded by

L_{k}\vee\log B_{k}\lesssim\Gamma_{{\mathcal{M}},q_{\rm geom}}^{C}B_{0}^{C}(D\vee\log n)^{\mathfrak{p}_{\beta,d}}
and
\|{\bm{W}}_{k}\|_{\infty}\vee S_{k}\lesssim\Gamma_{{\mathcal{M}},q_{\rm geom}}^{C}B_{0}^{C}p_{\min}^{-C}(\sigma_{t_{k-1}}^{-d}+r_{\star,n}^{-d})(D\vee\log n)^{\mathfrak{p}_{\beta,d}}
.

3.5
Small Noise: Projection-Centered Laplace Approximation

At small noise, extending tangent cells to
t_{0}
would be statistically too costly, so we center at the nearest projection. For
{\bm{x}}\in A_{t}
and
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
, the reach condition makes
\Pi_{{\mathcal{M}}}({\bm{x}}/m_{t})
unique. Let
H\coloneqq D\vee\log n
and define

{\bm{\pi}}({\bm{x}},t)\coloneqq\Pi_{{\mathcal{M}}}({\bm{x}}/m_{t}),\qquad{\bm{u}}_{i}^{\Pi}\coloneqq\phi_{i}({\bm{\pi}}({\bm{x}},t)),\qquad{\bm{r}}\coloneqq{\bm{x}}-m_{t}{\bm{\pi}}({\bm{x}},t),\qquad{\bm{\nu}}\coloneqq{\bm{r}}/\sigma_{t}.

Then
\nabla{\bm{z}}_{i}({\bm{u}}_{i}^{\Pi})^{\top}{\bm{r}}=0
, so the normal residual is separated before the tangential Laplace expansion.

By introducing the local parameterization
{\bm{u}}={\bm{u}}_{i}^{\Pi}+\sigma_{t}{\bm{w}}
, the phase expansion rigorously yields:

\frac{\|{\bm{x}}-m_{t}{\bm{z}}_{i}({\bm{u}}_{i}^{\Pi}+\sigma_{t}{\bm{w}})\|_{2}^{2}-\|{\bm{r}}\|_{2}^{2}}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}=\frac{m_{t}^{2}}{2}{\bm{w}}^{\top}{\bm{G}}_{i}({\bm{u}}_{i}^{\Pi}){\bm{w}}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\lfloor\beta\rfloor}\sigma_{t}^{\ell}\Psi_{i,\ell}({\bm{u}}_{i}^{\Pi},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{w}},t)+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma_{t}^{\beta}).

The leading term is a
d
-dimensional Gaussian in
{\bm{w}}
.
To remove the unstable normal factor, define

\bar{\mathcal{Q}}\coloneqq e^{\|{\bm{r}}\|_{2}^{2}/(2\sigma_{t}^{2})}{\mathcal{Q}},\qquad\bar{\mathcal{U}}\coloneqq e^{\|{\bm{r}}\|_{2}^{2}/(2\sigma_{t}^{2})}{\mathcal{U}},\qquad\frac{\bar{\mathcal{U}}}{\bar{\mathcal{Q}}}=\frac{{\mathcal{U}}}{{\mathcal{Q}}}.

The denominator now has the polynomial floor

\bar{\mathcal{Q}}({\bm{x}},t)\geq C^{-1}\Gamma_{{\mathcal{M}},q_{\rm geom}}^{-C}p_{\min}(D\vee\log n)^{-d/4}
. The remaining geometric task is to realize the projection center and hence the coordinate
{\bm{u}}_{i}^{\Pi}({\bm{x}},t)
without treating
\Pi_{{\mathcal{M}}}
as a black-box
D
-variate smooth map.

Lemma 3.3

(Informal projection-network primitive; see
Theorems

F.13
and
F.23
)
.

On the active reach tube used in the formal small-noise theorem, for every accuracy
\varepsilon_{\Pi}\in(0,1]
, there are ReLU networks approximating
\Pi_{{\mathcal{M}}}({\bm{x}}/m_{t})
and its chart coordinates
{\bm{u}}_{i}^{\Pi}({\bm{x}},t)=\phi_{i}(\Pi_{{\mathcal{M}}}({\bm{x}}/m_{t}))
.
The construction uses only intrinsic nonlinear approximation: it fixes finitely many anchors in each
d
-dimensional chart box, unrolls approximate Gauss–Newton steps

{\bm{u}}\mapsto\Pi_{i}^{({\rm box})}\!\left({\bm{u}}+{\bm{A}}_{i}({\bm{u}})({\bm{x}}/m_{t}-{\bm{z}}_{i}({\bm{u}}))\right),\qquad{\bm{A}}_{i}({\bm{u}})=({\bm{J}}_{i}({\bm{u}})^{\top}{\bm{J}}_{i}({\bm{u}}))^{-1}{\bm{J}}_{i}({\bm{u}})^{\top},

and then combines all chart-anchor candidates by a ReLU objective gate based on
\|{\bm{x}}/m_{t}-{\bm{z}}_{i}({\bm{u}})\|_{2}^{2}
.
If
A_{{\mathcal{M}}}
is the finite intrinsic-anchor count and
C_{\rm proj}
is the geometry constant from
Theorem

F.13
, the dominant width/sparsity cost is

A_{{\mathcal{M}}}D\bigl(C_{\rm proj}D\varepsilon_{\Pi}^{-2}\bigr)^{d/(2(\beta\vee 1))}{\mathrm{Polylog}}(C_{\rm proj}D\varepsilon_{\Pi}^{-1}),

with depth and log-weight polylogarithmic in
C_{\rm proj}D\varepsilon_{\Pi}^{-1}
.

Thus, the projection step has intrinsic exponent and explicit polynomial ambient dependence under polynomial-geometry control, rather than a generic
\varepsilon_{\Pi}^{-D/\tilde{\beta}}
ambient smooth-function cost.

Proposition 3.4

(Informal version of
Corollary

F.29
)
.

Under
1
,
2
and
3
, the polynomial-growth condition
D\vee p_{\min}^{-1}\leq n^{a_{0}}
, and the small-noise reach and denominator-resolution checks
Equations

231
and
232
, there exists a clipped ReLU network
s_{\rm sm}^{\rm lb}\in{\mathcal{NN}}(L_{\rm sm},{\bm{W}}_{\rm sm},S_{\rm sm},B_{\rm sm})
active over
[t_{0},t_{1}]
such that:

\|s_{\rm sm}^{\rm lb}({\bm{x}},t)-s^{*}({\bm{x}},t)\|_{2}^{2}\leq C\Gamma_{{\mathcal{M}},q_{\rm geom}}^{C}B_{0}^{C}p_{\min}^{-C}n^{-2\beta/(d+2\beta)}(D\vee\log n)^{\mathfrak{p}_{\beta,d}}\sigma_{t}^{-2},\;\;{\bm{x}}\in A_{t},\;t\in[t_{0},t_{1}].

Moreover,
\sup_{{\bm{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}\|s_{\rm sm}^{\rm lb}({\bm{x}},t)\|_{\infty}\leq C\sigma_{t}^{-1}(D\vee\log n)^{1/2}
,
L_{\rm sm}\vee\log B_{\rm sm}\lesssim\Gamma_{{\mathcal{M}},q_{\rm geom}}^{C}B_{0}^{C}p_{\min}^{-C}(D\vee\log n)^{\mathfrak{p}_{\beta,d}}
and
\|{\bm{W}}_{\rm sm}\|_{\infty}\vee S_{\rm sm}\lesssim\Gamma_{{\mathcal{M}},q_{\rm geom}}^{C}B_{0}^{C}p_{\min}^{-C}n^{d/(d+2\beta)}(D\vee\log n)^{\mathfrak{p}_{\beta,d}}
.

The two approximation branches are assembled statistically, rather than by a separate deterministic theorem in the main text.
In
Section

4
, the small-noise projection–Laplace network is trained once on
[t_{0},t_{1}]
, while the large-noise tangent-cell networks are trained on dyadic slabs over
[t_{1},T]
and combined by ReLU time switches.
The formal learned-score assembly is
Theorems

G.3
and
G.2
, and the final
{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
telescoping step is
Corollary

H.1
.

4
Score Estimation on Smooth Manifolds

Recall the rate notation
\mathfrak{r}_{n}
and
q_{\star}
from
Equation

3
; the large-noise accuracy radius
r_{\star,n}
is defined in
Proposition

G.2
.
This section turns the comparison networks from
Section

3
into learned-score bounds. The two displays below are the main-text informal versions of
Theorems

G.3
and
G.2
.
On
[t_{0},t_{1}]
, clipped ERM over the projection–Laplace class gives

{\mathbb{E}}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bm{X}}_{t}}\|\widehat{s}_{\rm sm}^{\rm lb}({\bm{X}}_{t},t)-s^{*}({\bm{X}}_{t},t)\|_{2}^{2}{\mathrm{d}t}\leq\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}\!\Bigl(\Gamma_{{\mathcal{M}},q_{\rm geom}}^{C}B_{0}^{C}p_{\min}^{-C}n^{-2\beta/(d+2\beta)}D^{{\mathcal{O}}_{\beta}(d)}\Bigr).

On each large-noise slab
I_{k}=[t_{k-1},t_{k}]
, clipped ERM over the tangent-cell class gives

{\mathbb{E}}\int_{I_{k}}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bm{X}}_{t}}\|\widehat{s}_{k}^{\rm lg}({\bm{X}}_{t},t)-s^{*}({\bm{X}}_{t},t)\|_{2}^{2}{\mathrm{d}t}\leq\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}\!\Bigl(\Gamma_{{\mathcal{M}},q_{\rm geom}}^{C}B_{0}^{C}p_{\min}^{-C}\Bigl[n^{-2(\beta+1)/(d+2\beta)}+\frac{\sigma_{t_{k-1}}^{-d}+\mathfrak{r}_{n}^{-d/q_{\star}}}{n}\Bigr]D^{{\mathcal{O}}_{\beta}(d)}\Bigr).

These are the learned-score inputs used in
Section

5
.

Clipped ERM oracle and covering number bounds.

The formal proof is in
Appendix

G
.
We minimize the interval-restricted denoising score-matching loss, control the true smoothed risk
{\mathcal{L}}_{I}
, and clip learned scores at the tube-scale envelope
\sigma_{t}^{-1}(D\vee\log n)^{1/2}
.

For a slab
I=[{\underline{t}},{\overline{t}}]
, the notation
\widetilde{{\mathcal{NN}}}(L,{\bm{W}},S,B)
denotes the following clipped, compact-input version of the sparse ReLU class.
First apply the coordinatewise affine truncation
({\bm{x}},t)\mapsto(\Pi_{[-B_{x},B_{x}]^{D}}{\bm{x}},\Pi_{[{\underline{t}},{\overline{t}}]}t)
, with
B_{x}=(D\vee\log n)^{C}\sigma_{{\underline{t}}}^{-1}
; then rescale this compact rectangle to
[0,1]^{D+1}
, apply a network in
{\mathcal{NN}}(L,{\bm{W}},S,B)
, and finally clip each output coordinate to
C\sigma_{t}^{-1}(D\vee\log n)^{1/2}
.
The off-tube estimates make the discarded input tails negligible, and the compact rescaling is exactly the one used in the covering-number step of the oracle proof.

The clipped ERM oracle inequality (
Theorem

G.1
) balances approximation and complexity:

{\mathbb{E}}{\mathcal{L}}_{I}(\widehat{s})\lesssim\inf_{s\in\mathcal{F}}{\mathcal{L}}_{I}(s)+\frac{(D\vee\log n)^{2}\int_{I}\sigma_{t}^{-2}{\mathrm{d}t}\,\log\bigl({\mathcal{N}}(n^{-{\mathcal{O}}(1)},\mathcal{F},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\vee n\bigr)}{n}+n^{-A}.

For sparse ReLU networks with structural size
M
, the concrete entropy evaluation is: whenever

L\vee\log B\leq C_{\mathcal{F}}(D\vee\log n)^{C}
and

S\vee\|{\bm{W}}\|_{\infty}\leq C_{\mathcal{F}}M(D\vee\log n)^{C}
,
Lemma

I.14
gives

\log\bigl({\mathcal{N}}(n^{-{\mathcal{O}}(1)},\widetilde{\mathcal{NN}}(L,{\bm{W}},S,B),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\vee n\bigr)\leq CC_{\mathcal{F}}M(D\vee\log n)^{C}.

(8)

On our grids,
\int_{I}\sigma_{t}^{-2}{\mathrm{d}t}
is logarithmic or bounded, so the stochastic capacity penalty is
\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(M/n)
up to the displayed geometry factors in
C_{\mathcal{F}}
.

Global noise grid assembly.

The small-noise estimator is trained once on
[t_{0},t_{1}]
with entropy size
M_{\rm sm}=n^{d/(d+2\beta)}
.
The large-noise estimators are trained on dyadic slabs with radius
r_{k}=c_{0}\sigma_{t_{k-1}}\wedge r_{\star,n}
and then merged by ReLU time switches. The
{\mathcal{O}}(\log n)
switches add only logarithmic overhead, while the assembled score keeps both
\sigma_{t_{k-1}}^{-d}
and
r_{\star,n}^{-d}
explicit.

5
Distribution Estimation in
{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
Distance

This section converts the learned-score bounds from
Section

4
into the final distributional guarantee.
The formal slabwise
{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
perturbation and telescoping argument are in
Appendix

H
.

Set
t_{0}\asymp n^{-2(\beta+1)/(d+2\beta)},t_{1}\asymp n^{-2/(d+2\beta)},T\asymp\log n
, and use the dyadic large-noise grid from
Equation

246
.
Then
\sqrt{t_{0}}\asymp\mathfrak{r}_{n}
,
\sigma_{t_{1}}\asymp n^{-1/(d+2\beta)}
, and the terminal Gaussian initialization error is negligible.
Let
\widehat{s}^{\rm lb}
be the switched clipped estimator obtained by combining the small-noise and large-noise ERM estimators, and let
\widehat{P}_{t_{0}}^{\rm lb}
be the law at time
t_{0}
of the reverse VP process driven by
\widehat{s}^{\rm lb}
.

The rate assembly has three terms. Early stopping gives
{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{Dt_{0}})
, which is absorbed by the displayed ambient polynomial times
\mathfrak{r}_{n}
, while the terminal error is
{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{D}e^{-cT})
and is made negligible by choosing
T\asymp\log n
with a sufficiently large constant.
On the small-noise interval, the score risk
\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(n^{-2\beta/(d+2\beta)})
is
multiplied by the perturbation width
\sqrt{t_{1}}
, giving

\sqrt{t_{1}}\,n^{-\beta/(d+2\beta)}\asymp n^{-(\beta+1)/(d+2\beta)}=\mathfrak{r}_{n}.

On the large-noise slabs, the stochastic score term contains
(\sigma_{t_{k-1}}^{-d}+\mathfrak{r}_{n}^{-d/q_{\star}})/n
.
The dyadic part contributes

n^{-1/2}\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq 1}t_{k}^{1/2}\sigma_{t_{k-1}}^{-d/2}\lesssim n^{-1/2}\bigl(1+\sigma_{t_{1}}^{1-d/2}\bigr)\lesssim n^{-(\beta+1)/(d+2\beta)}+n^{-1/2}.

The order-one-time slabs add only another
\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(n^{-1/2})
term, and the
finite-order accuracy cap contributes
n^{-1/2}\mathfrak{r}_{n}^{-d/(2q_{\star})}
.

Regime for the main guarantee.

For the statement below, assume the manifold and density conditions
1
,
2
and
3
, the
polynomial control
D\vee p_{\min}^{-1}\leq n^{a_{0}}
, the time grid above, the canonical
finite-order choice
q_{\rm geom}=q_{\rm opt}(\beta)
, and the two small-noise sample-size checks
Equations

231
and
232
.

Section

H.1
records these conditions in checklist form
and explains that, for a fixed well-conditioned manifold and fixed
p_{\min}>0
, the
small-noise checks hold for all sufficiently large
n
.
The formal version, including network-size bounds, is
Corollary

H.1
.

Theorem 5.1

(Main
{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
guarantee; informal version of
Corollary

H.1
)
.

Under the preceding regime, the switched clipped VP-SGM estimator satisfies

{\mathbb{E}}\,{\mathsf{W}}_{1}(P_{0},\widehat{P}_{t_{0}}^{\rm lb})\leq\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}\!\bigl(\Gamma_{{\mathcal{M}},q_{\rm geom}}^{C}B_{0}^{C}p_{\min}^{-C}(D\vee\log n)^{\mathfrak{p}_{\beta,d}}\bigl[\mathfrak{r}_{n}+n^{-1/2}+n^{-1/2}\mathfrak{r}_{n}^{-d/(2q_{\star})}\bigr]\bigr),

where
q_{\star}
and
\mathfrak{r}_{n}
are from
Equation

3
.
If
d>2
, then
q_{\star}\geq d(\beta+1)/(d-2)
, both bracketed correction terms are
{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{r}_{n})
, and
\mathfrak{p}_{\beta,d}={\mathcal{O}}_{\beta}(d)
, so the bound simplifies to

{\mathbb{E}}\,{\mathsf{W}}_{1}(P_{0},\widehat{P}_{t_{0}}^{\rm lb})\leq\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}\!\bigl(\Gamma_{{\mathcal{M}},q_{\rm geom}}^{C}B_{0}^{C}p_{\min}^{-C}D^{{\mathcal{O}}_{\beta}(d)}n^{-(\beta+1)/(d+2\beta)}\bigr).

The exponent
\mathfrak{p}_{\beta,d}
and the non-log geometry and density prefactors are made explicit in
Corollary

H.1
.
For fixed geometry and fixed density lower bound, the
d>2
display matches the intrinsic minimax
{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
sample exponent in the fixed-geometry sense.
A sufficient ambient-growth condition for the two small-noise checks is given in
Corollary

H.2
.

6
Conclusion

This paper proves an intrinsic
{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
guarantee for a VP-SGM estimator on compact smooth manifolds by separating the score approximation into large-noise tangent cells and small-noise projection-centered Laplace expansions. The large-noise branch has explicit capacity
\sigma_{t_{k-1}}^{-d}+r_{\star,n}^{-d}
; the small-noise branch avoids a
\sigma_{t_{0}}
-scale mesh by de-Gaussianizing the ratio and computing projection coordinates through finite-anchor Gauss–Newton networks in local
d
-dimensional frames.

Under the stated reach, density, denominator, and finite-geometry conditions, the learned reverse process obeys
n^{-(\beta+1)/(d+2\beta)}+n^{-1/2}n^{\frac{d(\beta+1)}{2q_{\star}(d+2\beta)}}
up to logarithmic, geometry, density, and explicit ambient factors. For
d>2
, the canonical finite-order choice makes the cap
{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-(\beta+1)/(d+2\beta)})
, so the sample exponent matches the intrinsic minimax
{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
exponent in the fixed-geometry sense.
The proof remains conditional on exact support, a positive density lower bound, and explicit small-noise admissibility conditions; it also studies an idealized slabwise estimator rather than shared-parameter diffusion training.
Open problems include relaxing those support and density assumptions, adding ambient observational noise, and analyzing optimization and discretization error.

Appendix A
More Discussions of Related Work

A.1
Detailed Comparison with Concurrent Work

Although the concurrent study by
[
58
]
shares our objective of establishing curvature-driven statistical bounds for diffusion models on nonlinear manifolds, the technical difference mainly lies in how the nearest-point projection
\Pi_{{\mathcal{M}}}
is made dimension-explicit inside the proof.

Specifically,
[
58
, Lemma D.18]
invokes a generic ambient smooth-function approximation step for
\Pi_{{\mathcal{M}}}
on the reach tube. Our contribution is to replace that step by a constructive intrinsic-coordinate implementation. The comparison below therefore concerns the ambient audit of one standard black-box instantiation of the smooth-map step, not an impossibility claim for every realization of the concurrent theorem.

To see the ambient bookkeeping issue, suppose one approximates
\Pi_{{\mathcal{M}}}
by a standard
D
-variate
C^{\tilde{\beta}}
ReLU approximation theorem at the balancing scale needed to recover intrinsic complexity. In the relevant regime, the effective order is roughly
\tilde{\beta}\gtrsim D\beta/d
, up to logarithmic and auxiliary-accuracy factors. In a local Taylor formulation, approximating order
\tilde{\beta}
requires a combinatorial number of ambient mixed partial derivatives:

\binom{D+\lceil D\beta/d\rceil}{\lceil D\beta/d\rceil}=\exp\!\left(D\{(1+\beta/d)\log(1+\beta/d)-(\beta/d)\log(\beta/d)\}+{\mathcal{O}}(\log D)\right).

At the balancing scale
\tilde{\beta}\asymp D\beta/d
, this monomial count already scales exponentially as
\exp(c_{\beta,d}D)
for fixed intrinsic parameters
d
and
\beta
.

Furthermore, if the projection map’s derivative envelope grows proportionally with the differentiation order (for example, if the
\tilde{\beta}
-th derivative is bounded by
C^{\tilde{\beta}}
), this black-box route can incur an additional exponential-in-
D
capacity penalty. Our point is therefore constructive: a generic ambient smooth-function approximation of
\Pi_{{\mathcal{M}}}
comes with a nontrivial dimension-explicit audit, whereas our proof avoids that route entirely.

By contrast, our architecture avoids high-order ambient Taylor expansions for the projection map. We evaluate the projection center dynamically using finite-anchor Gauss–Newton iterations restricted entirely to
d
-dimensional local chart coordinates, and then obtain active chart coordinates by affine projection charts. Coupled with an analytical de-Gaussianization step that enforces a polynomial lower bound prior to the reciprocal network evaluation, our method keeps the ambient dependence of the controlled network parameters explicit and polynomial in
D
in the polynomial-geometry regime covered by our theorems.
These distinctions affect the dimension-explicit prefactors rather than the sample exponent itself, and are summarized in
Table

2
.

Table 2:
Comparison of our work to the concurrent study
[
58
]
.

Technical Challenge

[
58
]

This Paper

Projection Mechanism

Uses a generic ambient smooth-function approximation step for
\Pi_{{\mathcal{M}}}
on the reach tube.

Computes
\Pi_{{\mathcal{M}}}
dynamically via finite-anchor optimization restricted to
d
-dimensional intrinsic charts.

Approximation Strategy

The published proof leaves the projection-step ambient audit to an ambient smooth-function approximation; under a standard black-box implementation, the relevant order is roughly
\tilde{\beta}\gtrsim D\beta/d
, up to logarithmic factors.

Resolves projection candidates via Gauss–Newton iterations in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and obtains chart coordinates by affine maps, naturally achieving the intrinsic
\varepsilon^{-d/\beta}
covering complexity.

Dimensionality Bottleneck

A standard high-order ambient implementation would involve a combinatorial number of mixed partial derivatives:
\binom{D+\tilde{\beta}}{\tilde{\beta}}
, which is
\exp(c_{\beta,d}D)
when
\tilde{\beta}\asymp D\beta/d
.

Utilizes strictly finite-order geometry (with
q_{\rm geom}
fixed before the limit); isolates ambient powers into
(D\vee\log n)^{\mathfrak{p}_{\beta,d}}
, summarized as
D^{{\mathcal{O}}_{\beta}(d)}
in the main
d>2
regime.

Denominator Stability

Handles the sharp normal Gaussian singularity through the same projection-centered smooth-map step.

Explicitly de-Gaussianizes the integrals, guaranteeing a stable polynomial lower bound prior to evaluating the reciprocal network.

Complexity Guarantee

Displays the intrinsic sample exponent, while the dimension-explicit prefactors depend on how the projection step is instantiated.

Keeps network parameters polynomial in
D
under the polynomial-geometry conditions of the formal theorem.

A.2
Comparison with the Support-aware
\sqrt{D}
Result

The recent paper
[
1
]
proves a
\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(\sqrt{D}n^{-(\alpha+1)/(2\alpha+d)+\gamma})
Wasserstein bound under the manifold hypothesis. This is a strong ambient factor, but it is obtained for a different estimator architecture from the one studied here.

Their first key step is the score-learning target itself. In Theorem 5 of
[
1
]
, the main bound is for the weighted quantity

\int\sigma_{t}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\|\widehat{s}_{t}-s_{t}\|_{2}^{2}\,dt
,
rather than the unweighted score loss that enters our slabwise
{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
perturbation argument. This weighting downplays the most singular part of the small-noise regime. The displayed
\sqrt{D}
factor then appears in their Corollary 6 through the final early-stopping conversion from score error to
{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
.

Their second key step is structural. They first estimate the support by a sample-dependent piecewise polynomial manifold surrogate
M^{\ast}
, then use Proposition 21 to show that each local polynomial piece lies in an affine span of dimension
{\mathcal{O}}(\log n)
. Section 3.1 of
[
1
]
explicitly states that this reduces the ambient score problem to
N
simpler
{\mathcal{O}}(\log n)
-dimensional subproblems. The resulting estimator class is therefore support-aware: the local centers and subspaces are extracted from the data before the score network is fit. Indeed, their construction notes that these geometric quantities are found directly from the sample rather than optimized jointly within a generic ambient network.

This distinction matters for the ambient-dimension comparison. Our theorem does not preprocess the manifold into sample-dependent low-rank affine spans. Instead, it analyzes a fully explicit ambient score network that directly computes the nearest-projection center, its active chart coordinates, and the de-Gaussianized denominator in the small-noise regime. That is a harder constructive problem, so one should not expect the same ambient factor. On the other hand, the result here gives a theorem-level audit of the entire projection-centered score construction and attains the exact intrinsic sample exponent without an extra
n^{\gamma}
slack.

The difference is also visible inside
[
1
]
itself. Their Theorem 7(i) proves the dimension-light rate for the structured support-aware class, whereas Theorem 7(ii) shows that when this estimator is flattened into a more standard ambient ReLU architecture, an explicit factor of
D
reappears in the score-learning bound. We therefore view the two analyses as complementary:
[
1
]
obtains the sharper displayed ambient prefactor for a support-aware pipeline, while our theorem certifies a less structured ambient-network realization of the manifold score.

A.3
Additional Related Work

While our work focuses on the statistical guarantees of
learning
the score function, a broad body of literature addresses other theoretical aspects of diffusion models.

Convergence analysis on ambient space.

Early theoretical works focused on establishing convergence guarantees for data distributions supported on the full ambient space
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
.
Initial analyses often relied on stringent structural assumptions, such as Log-Sobolev Inequalities (LSI) or log-concavity, to derive polynomial convergence rates
[
26
,
53
,
5
]
.
Subsequent research sought to relax these conditions to better model complex, multi-modal data.
[
7
,
27
]
demonstrated that
L^{2}
-accurate score estimation is sufficient for polynomial convergence without requiring functional inequalities, extending guarantees to distributions with bounded support or decaying tails.
More recently, the focus has shifted to minimal assumptions and faster rates.
[
8
]
showed that finite Fisher information is sufficient for convergence without early stopping.

[
2
]
utilized stochastic localization to prove that convergence rates scale linearly with the ambient dimension
D
(up to logarithmic factors), while
[
32
,
31
]
provided faster non-asymptotic convergence rates (e.g.,
{\mathcal{O}}(D/T)
) for both ODE and SDE-based samplers.

[
21
]
further refined these results for DDPMs, establishing near-optimal rates under relaxed smoothness conditions.
Despite these advances, ambient space analyses inherently depend on
D
, often yielding vacuous bounds when applied to singular measures supported on low-dimensional manifolds (
d\ll D
), as they do not exploit the intrinsic geometry of the data.

Convergence analysis on low-dimensional structures.

Recently,
[
19
]
and
[
35
]
concurrently proved that the iteration complexity of Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models (DDPM) scales linearly with the intrinsic dimension
d
, rather than the ambient dimension
D
, establishing the optimal adaptivity of stochastic samplers.
Similarly, for deterministic samplers,
[
47
]
and
[
24
]
showed that Probability Flow ODEs and DDIMs achieve dimension-free convergence rates in TV distance.

[
17
]
further analyzed the dynamics of these processes, identifying phase transitions (speciation and collapse) dependent on the dimension ratios.
Our work complements these findings by addressing the upstream problem: statistically learning the score function from finite data.

Intrinsic-dimension statistical guarantees.

Two very recent works develop complementary intrinsic-dimensional viewpoints.

[
6
]
studies score-matching diffusion models for general low-dimensional measures through a
(p,q)
-Wasserstein dimension, building on empirical-measure Wasserstein rates such as
[
51
]
. Their theory yields broad
{\mathsf{W}}_{p}
bounds of order roughly
n^{-1/d^{\star}_{p,q}(\mu)}
up to logarithmic and algorithmic approximation terms, and it applies beyond smooth compact manifolds under finite-moment conditions. This generality is different from our smooth-density setting: for a
d
-dimensional compact manifold with
\beta
-Hölder density and
d>2
, the smoothness-adaptive minimax
{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
exponent is
(\beta+1)/(d+2\beta)
, which is faster than the empirical-measure exponent
1/d
.
[
28
]
also studies finite-sample manifold diffusion from empirical data and uses an inertial correction to avoid memorization, obtaining an ambient-dimension-free
{\mathsf{W}}_{1}
guarantee for
C^{2}
manifolds. Our analysis instead learns a population score over neural-network classes for Hölder densities and audits the concrete projection–Laplace construction, including the de-Gaussianized small-noise denominator and the nearest-projection network.

Diffusion dynamics and schedules.

The choice of the forward diffusion process and its noise schedule plays a central role in the performance of score-based generative models.

[
41
]
provided error bounds explicitly depending on the noise schedule, highlighting the impact of schedule tuning.

[
25
,
57
]
explored algorithmic accelerations, achieving faster convergence rates through higher-order solvers and randomized midpoint methods.
Regarding the optimal process choice,
[
4
]
analyzed the forward diffusion and argued that the canonical OU process is hard to beat in terms of convergence to the invariant measure.

Geometric interpretations.

Beyond statistical rates, recent works have explored how SGMs encode geometry.
[
36
]
gave conditions under which SGMs detect the underlying data manifold, even when the on-manifold density is not recovered accurately.
[
38
]
showed that diffusion models approximate the normal bundle of the data manifold, enabling intrinsic dimension estimation.

[
54
]
framed diffusion training as subspace clustering, showing that optimizing the diffusion loss effectively learns mixtures of low-rank Gaussians.

[
30
]
and
[
33
]
analyze the singular structure of Euclidean diffusion scores near embedded manifolds, including the normal–tangential scale separation that motivates modified sampling or training strategies.

[
29
]
gives a particularly sharp small-noise perspective: geometric information in the score appears at order
\sigma^{-2}
, whereas density information appears at order one. Their results show that manifold concentration or uniform geometric sampling can tolerate substantially larger score errors than exact distributional recovery. Relatedly,
[
13
]
studies maximum-entropy exploration over a diffusion-defined data manifold via fine-tuning. Our theorem addresses the harder distribution-learning target; correspondingly, the small-noise projection–Laplace branch controls the lower-order density-dependent terms rather than only the leading normal projection field.
Finally,
[
23
]
observed that diffusion denoisers exhibit increasing linearity during the generalization phase, approximating optimal linear denoisers.

B.4
Standard manifold conventions

We recall the elementary differential-geometric conventions used throughout the
appendix, mainly to make the proof self-contained for readers who do not use this
notation daily.

Definition B.1

(Chart)
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a
d
-dimensional Riemannian manifold isometrically embedded in

{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
. A chart on
{\mathcal{M}}
is a pair
(U,\phi)
, where
U\subset{\mathcal{M}}
is open and

\phi:U\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is a homeomorphism onto its image.

Two charts
(U,\phi)
and
(V,\psi)
are
C^{k}
compatible if the transition maps

\phi\circ\psi^{-1}
and
\psi\circ\phi^{-1}
are
C^{k}
on their common coordinate
domains.

Definition B.2

(
C^{k}
atlas)
.

A
C^{k}
atlas for
{\mathcal{M}}
is a collection of pairwise
C^{k}
compatible charts

\{(U_{i},\phi_{i})\}_{i\in{\mathcal{A}}}
such that
\bigcup_{i\in{\mathcal{A}}}U_{i}={\mathcal{M}}
.

Definition B.3

(Smooth manifold)
.

A smooth manifold is a manifold equipped with a
C^{\infty}
atlas.

Definition B.4

(Partition of unity)
.

A
C^{\infty}
partition of unity on
{\mathcal{M}}
is a collection of nonnegative

C^{\infty}
functions
\{\rho_{i}:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\}_{i\in{\mathcal{A}}}
such that the supports are
locally finite and
\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{A}}}\rho_{i}=1
on
{\mathcal{M}}
.

Every smooth manifold admits smooth partitions of unity subordinate to open covers

[
45
, Theorem 13.7]
. In the proof we use a finite partition
subordinate to the reach-scale projection atlas constructed in

Section

D.3
; multiplying by these cutoffs localizes manifold
integrals to single coordinate charts while preserving the finite smoothness needed in
the Hölder and Taylor estimates.

B.5
Notation and bookkeeping conventions

The conventions here supplement the global notation in
Section

2.1
only for
proof bookkeeping. Unadorned constants
C,c>0
remain
universal. Named or subscripted constants, such as
C_{\rm proj}
,
C_{Q}
, or

C_{\rm size,sm}
, may depend on the fixed objects named in the statement where they are
introduced, but not on
n,D,\sigma_{t}
or a tolerance parameter unless that dependence is
displayed. The notation
\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(\cdot)
hides logarithmic factors in displayed
sample-size, ambient, time-cutoff, and tolerance scales; it does not hide the density
lower bound, reach, volume, or finite-order geometry factors unless this is stated
explicitly.

Local approximation and estimation tolerances use descriptive subscripts; no global
sample-size tolerance is introduced. We write the smallest large-noise noise scale as

\sigma_{t_{1}}\asymp n^{-1/(d+2\beta)}
and the centered-residual scale as

\mathfrak{r}_{n}=n^{-(\beta+1)/(d+2\beta)}
. The large-noise intrinsic cell radius is not
identified with
\sigma_{t_{1}}
: on a slab
I_{k}=[t_{k-1},t_{k}]
it is

r_{k}=c_{0}\sigma_{t_{k-1}}\wedge r_{\star,n}
, where
r_{\star,n}
is the
accuracy-limited radius defined in
Proposition

G.2
. Thus,
when the cap is active, the complexity records both
\sigma_{t_{k-1}}^{-d}
and

r_{\star,n}^{-d}
explicitly.

The symbols
\delta
,
\Delta
, and
\eta
are reserved for geometric quantities such as
active radii, displacements, and chart/projection margins. The tube radius

\varrho_{t}
, the generic physical density floor
\rho_{t}
, and the partition-of-unity
weights
\rho_{i}
are distinct. Under the density lower bound, denominator floors used
inside reciprocal networks are denoted locally.

In intermediate appendix estimates,
\mathfrak{a}_{D},\mathfrak{a}_{\log}\geq 1
denote fixed
finite bookkeeping exponents for direct ambient powers and shell/covering factors, while

\mathfrak{a}_{\rm geom}\geq 1
denotes a fixed exponent for the displayed geometry factors

1\vee S_{{\mathcal{M}}}
,
1\vee\kappa^{-1}
, and
\mathcal{K}_{{\mathcal{M}},q_{\rm geom}}
. These
exponents never depend on
D,n,\sigma_{t},\varepsilon_{\rm rel},p_{\min}
or on the
numerical values of the geometry constants. The appendix analysis interval is denoted by

[{\underline{t}},{\overline{t}}]
. When a proof localizes to the high-probability tube,
H
or
H_{n}

denotes
D\vee\log n
unless another meaning is explicitly declared.
On a generic compact time interval,
\sigma_{{\underline{t}}}
always means

\inf_{t\in[{\underline{t}},{\overline{t}}]}\sigma_{t}
rather than a formal endpoint value; the corresponding
schedule lower- and upper-bound convention is stated explicitly in

Equation

11
.

B.6
Finite-order geometry and tube-volume conventions

Definition B.5

(Finite-order geometry convention)
.

Fix the reach-scale projection atlas used below. For each
q\geq 1
, let

\mathcal{K}_{{\mathcal{M}},q}\geq e
denote a finite high-order atlas envelope controlling the

C^{q+4}
norms of the inverse projection charts, transition maps, partition cutoffs,
Jacobian factors, the maps
{\bm{A}}_{i}={\bm{G}}_{i}^{-1}{\bm{J}}_{i}^{\top}
, and the nearest-projection
coordinate and residual maps on the compact reach tubes where they are used. The finite
geometry constant
\Gamma_{{\mathcal{M}},q}
in the approximation proofs may be chosen so that

\Gamma_{{\mathcal{M}},q}\leq C_{d,q}(1\vee S_{{\mathcal{M}}})^{\mathfrak{a}_{\rm geom}(q)}(1\vee\kappa^{-1})^{\mathfrak{a}_{\rm geom}(q)}\mathcal{K}_{{\mathcal{M}},q}^{\mathfrak{a}_{\rm geom}(q)},

where
\mathfrak{a}_{\rm geom}(q)
depends only on
d
and
q
. In final statements,

\mathfrak{a}_{\rm geom}
denotes this exponent at
q=q_{\rm geom}
, enlarged to absorb
the finite network-arithmetic powers of
\Gamma_{{\mathcal{M}},q_{\rm geom}}
. Reach alone does not
control
\mathcal{K}_{{\mathcal{M}},q}
, since the approximation uses finitely many derivatives of
charts and projection maps above second order.

Polynomial-geometry regime.

The phrase “polynomial ambient dependence” in the main paper is always conditional on
the displayed instance-dependent factors being polynomially controlled. Concretely, for
the fixed order
q_{\rm geom}
used in the final theorem, if there is an exponent

a_{\rm geom}
independent of
n
and
D
such that

S_{{\mathcal{M}}}\vee\kappa^{-1}\vee\mathcal{K}_{{\mathcal{M}},q_{\rm geom}}\leq D^{a_{\rm geom}},

then
\Gamma_{{\mathcal{M}},q_{\rm geom}}\leq D^{C_{\beta,d,a_{\rm geom}}}
and every network-size
bound displayed below has polynomial ambient dependence. This covers, for example,
families admitting reach-scale atlases with polynomially many uniformly well-conditioned
charts and polynomially bounded derivatives up to the fixed order
q_{\rm geom}+4
.
If these finite-order geometry envelopes grow exponentially with
D
, our statements
display that growth rather than hiding it in
\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(\cdot)
or in universal
constants.

B.7
Examples satisfying the polynomial-geometry regime

We record concrete geometric families for which the preceding polynomial regime is
verified. The statements below are only about the geometric factors

S_{{\mathcal{M}}},\kappa^{-1}
, and
\mathcal{K}_{{\mathcal{M}},q}
. The density assumptions in the main
theorem can then be imposed independently; for example, the uniform density on any of
the following manifolds has

p_{\min}^{-1}\leq 1\vee S_{{\mathcal{M}}}
and
B_{0}\leq 1\vee S_{{\mathcal{M}}}^{-1}
, so the density
factors are polynomially controlled in the examples below as well; the radius or
pseudoinverse hypotheses give the needed polynomial lower bound on
S_{{\mathcal{M}}}
.

Proposition B.6

(Basic polynomial-geometry examples)
.

Fix
d\geq 1
, a finite order
q\geq 1
, and set
q_{\rm emb}=q+6
. The following
families satisfy the polynomial-geometry regime for the reach-scale projection atlas in

Definition

B.5
. For each displayed manifold write

\kappa_{D}=\operatorname{reach}({\mathcal{M}}_{D})
. In (iii)–(iv),
C^{k}
norms of
ambient-valued maps use Euclidean target and operator norms in the fixed finite atlas.

(i)

Affine spheres.

Let
D\geq d+1
, let
E_{D}={\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}\times\{0\}^{D-d-1}
, let

{\bm{c}}_{D}=(1/2,\dots,1/2)
, and let
0<r_{D}\leq 1/4
. Set

{\mathcal{M}}_{D}=\{{\bm{c}}_{D}+r_{D}{\bm{u}}:{\bm{u}}\in E_{D},\ \|{\bm{u}}\|_{2}=1\}\subset[0,1]^{D}.

If
r_{D}^{-1}\leq D^{a}
, then

S_{{\mathcal{M}}_{D}}\vee\kappa_{D}^{-1}\vee\mathcal{K}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{D},q}\leq C_{d,q,a}D^{C_{d,q}a}.

(ii)

Flat product tori.

Let
D\geq 2d
, let
0<r_{\min,D}\leq r_{j,D}\leq 1/4
for

j=1,\dots,d
, define

\mathcal{C}_{j,D}:=\{(1/2+r_{j,D}\cos\theta,1/2+r_{j,D}\sin\theta):\theta\in[0,2\pi)\},

and set

{\mathcal{M}}_{D}=\mathcal{C}_{1,D}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}_{d,D}\times\{1/2\}^{D-2d}
.
If
r_{\min,D}^{-1}\leq D^{a}
, then

S_{{\mathcal{M}}_{D}}\vee\kappa_{D}^{-1}\vee\mathcal{K}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{D},q}\leq C_{d,q,a}D^{C_{d,q}a}.

(iii)

A finite-order embedding criterion.

Let
N
be a fixed compact smooth
d
-manifold without boundary, equipped with a
fixed finite atlas. Let
\Phi_{D}:N\to[0,1]^{D}
be a
C^{q_{\rm emb}}
embedding
and set
{\mathcal{M}}_{D}=\Phi_{D}(N)
. Suppose that, in the fixed atlas,

\|\Phi_{D}\|_{C^{q_{\rm emb}}(N)}\vee\|({\mathrm{d}}\Phi_{D})^{\dagger}\|_{C^{q_{\rm emb}-1}(N)}\vee\operatorname{reach}({\mathcal{M}}_{D})^{-1}\leq D^{a},

where
({\mathrm{d}}\Phi_{D})^{\dagger}
is the Moore–Penrose inverse of the differential in
local coordinates. Then

S_{{\mathcal{M}}_{D}}\vee\kappa_{D}^{-1}\vee\mathcal{K}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{D},q}\leq C_{N,d,q,a}D^{C_{N,d,q}a}.

(iv)

Closed graph-type manifolds.

As a special case of (iii), let
N
be a fixed closed
d
-manifold and let

\iota:N\to{\mathbb{R}}^{m_{0}}
be a fixed smooth embedding. For
D\geq m_{0}
, let

F_{D}:N\to{\mathbb{R}}^{D-m_{0}}
be a
C^{q_{\rm emb}}
map. Suppose

\|F_{D}\|_{C^{q_{\rm emb}}(N)}\leq D^{a}
and, for some
{\bm{\tau}}_{D}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
,

\Phi_{D}=\lambda_{D}(\iota,F_{D})+{\bm{\tau}}_{D}
maps
N
into
[0,1]^{D}
with

D^{-a}\leq\lambda_{D}\leq 1
and

\operatorname{reach}(\Phi_{D}(N))^{-1}\leq D^{a}.

Then
{\mathcal{M}}_{D}=\Phi_{D}(N)
satisfies the bound in (iii), with possibly larger constants.
Thus closed graph manifolds are covered when finite-order derivatives and reach are
polynomially controlled. A mere
C^{2}
curvature bound, without finite-order control
and a reach lower bound, is not sufficient for the present theorem.

Proof.

For (i), the reach is exactly
r_{D}
and

S_{{\mathcal{M}}_{D}}=r_{D}^{d}\mathrm{vol}({\mathbb{S}}^{d})
. The reach-scale tangent-projection charts are, up to
ambient rotations, the usual graph charts of a round sphere over tangent balls of radius

cr_{D}
; differentiating the graph formula shows that their inverse maps, transition
maps, and reach-scale cutoffs have
C^{q+4}
norms bounded by powers of
r_{D}^{-1}
. On
the
r_{D}/2
-tube,

\Pi_{{\mathcal{M}}_{D}}({\bm{x}})={\bm{c}}_{D}+r_{D}\frac{P_{E_{D}}({\bm{x}}-{\bm{c}}_{D})}{\|P_{E_{D}}({\bm{x}}-{\bm{c}}_{D})\|_{2}},

and the denominator is bounded below by
r_{D}/2
. Differentiating the displayed formula
up to order
q+4
gives

\mathcal{K}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{D},q}\leq C_{d,q}r_{D}^{-C_{d,q}}
.

For (ii), the reach is bounded below by
c_{d}r_{\min,D}
, and the nearest projection in
the corresponding product tube is obtained by normalizing each coordinate pair. The
volume is

S_{{\mathcal{M}}_{D}}=(2\pi)^{d}\prod_{j=1}^{d}r_{j,D}
. The reach-scale tangent-projection charts
are smooth changes of the product angular charts on angular intervals of length bounded
below by a constant multiple of
r_{\min,D}/r_{j,D}
, and quantitative inversion gives
finite-order chart, transition, and cutoff bounds by powers of
r_{\min,D}^{-1}
. The
coordinatewise projection formula

(x_{2j-1},x_{2j})\mapsto(1/2,1/2)+r_{j,D}\frac{(x_{2j-1}-1/2,x_{2j}-1/2)}{\|(x_{2j-1}-1/2,x_{2j}-1/2)\|_{2}},

has denominators bounded below by a constant multiple of
r_{\min,D}
. Hence

\mathcal{K}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{D},q}\leq C_{d,q}r_{\min,D}^{-C_{d,q}}
.

For (iii), the volume element in a fixed base chart is

\det(({\mathrm{d}}\Phi_{D})^{\top}{\mathrm{d}}\Phi_{D})^{1/2}
. The
C^{1}
bound on
\Phi_{D}
gives a
polynomial upper bound on every singular value of
{\mathrm{d}}\Phi_{D}
, while the pseudoinverse
bound gives a polynomial lower bound on the smallest singular value. Since the base
atlas on
N
is fixed, integrating over a fixed positive coordinate volume gives

S_{{\mathcal{M}}_{D}}\leq C_{N}D^{C_{d}a}
and
S_{{\mathcal{M}}_{D}}^{-1}\leq C_{N}D^{C_{d}a}
. The reach assumption gives

\kappa_{D}^{-1}\leq D^{a}
.

It remains to bound
\mathcal{K}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{D},q}
. Choose a maximal

c(1\wedge\kappa_{D})
-separated set of centers on
{\mathcal{M}}_{D}
. Standard reach-volume
estimates bound the number of centers by a fixed power of

(1\vee S_{{\mathcal{M}}_{D}})(1\vee\kappa_{D}^{-1})
. At a center

{\bm{y}}=\Phi_{D}(u_{0})
, use the tangent-projection map

u\mapsto P_{T_{{\bm{y}}}{\mathcal{M}}_{D}}(\Phi_{D}(u)-{\bm{y}})

in a base chart. Its derivative at
u_{0}
has smallest singular value at least

\|({\mathrm{d}}\Phi_{D})^{\dagger}\|^{-1}
, and its derivatives up to order
q_{\rm emb}
are
polynomially bounded. The reach chart lemma
Lemma

D.2
supplies the
reach-scale tangent-projection domains. On those domains, the tangent variation bound
in
Equation

14
and the pseudoinverse hypothesis give a
polynomial lower bound on the singular values of the coordinate differential. The
quantitative inverse-function theorem and the standard inverse-derivative formulas then
give coordinate maps, inverse maps, and transition maps with
C^{q+4}
norms bounded by

D^{C_{N,d,q}a}
. A scaled bump construction on the same cover gives partition cutoffs
with the same type of bound. Jacobian factors, Gram matrices, inverse Gram matrices,
and
{\bm{A}}_{i}={\bm{G}}_{i}^{-1}{\bm{J}}_{i}^{\top}
obey the same type of bound by repeated
differentiation of determinant and matrix-inverse formulas.

On the corresponding reach tube, the nearest projection is unique. In a tangent graph
chart
{\bm{z}}_{i}
, its local coordinate
{\bm{u}}^{\Pi}({\bm{\xi}})
solves

{\bm{J}}_{i}({\bm{u}})^{\top}({\bm{\xi}}-{\bm{z}}_{i}({\bm{u}}))=0.

The derivative of this equation with respect to
{\bm{u}}
equals the negative Gram matrix
plus a curvature-normal term. The tangent graph construction and the restriction to a
fixed numerical fraction of the reach tube make this derivative uniformly invertible,
with inverse norm bounded by
D^{C_{N,d,q}a}
. Applying the quantitative implicit
function theorem once more gives
C^{q+4}
bounds, polynomial in
D
, for the
projection-coordinate and residual maps. Therefore

\mathcal{K}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{D},q}\leq C_{N,d,q,a}D^{C_{N,d,q}a}
.

For (iv), the fixed embedding
\iota
gives a fixed lower singular-value bound for

{\mathrm{d}}\iota
. Since

\|\lambda_{D}({\mathrm{d}}\iota,{\mathrm{d}}F_{D})v\|_{2}\geq\lambda_{D}\|{\mathrm{d}}\iota\,v\|_{2}
and

\lambda_{D}\geq D^{-a}
, the pseudoinverse norm and its derivatives are polynomially
controlled by the
C^{q_{\rm emb}}
bound on
F_{D}
and matrix-inverse differentiation.
Thus the hypotheses of (iii) hold.
∎

Global tube-volume bound.

We use the following standard reach-volume consequence only for localization and geometry
bookkeeping. With
\omega_{k}=\pi^{k/2}/\Gamma(k/2+1)
and
\omega_{0}=1
, there is a
constant

C_{\rm tube}\leq C_{d}(1\vee S_{{\mathcal{M}}})(1\vee\kappa^{-1})^{d}

such that, for all
m\in(0,1]
and
r>0
,

\mathrm{vol}((m{\mathcal{M}})_{r})\leq C_{\rm tube}(1+r)^{d}\omega_{D-d}r^{D-d}.

(9)

This is the global tube bound used in the off-tube Gaussian tail estimates; the radius is
not required to be below the reach.

Appendix C
Off-Tube Score Localization

C.1
Off-manifold Gaussian tail and Fisher bounds

Lemma C.1

(Gaussian smoothing off-manifold deviation bound)
.

Let
{\bm{Y}}\sim P
be supported on a
d
-dimensional smooth manifold
{\mathcal{M}}\subseteq[0,1]^{D}
.
Fix
m>0
and
\sigma>0
, and define
{\bm{X}}=m{\bm{Y}}+\sigma{\bm{Z}}
, where
{\bm{Z}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}({\bm{0}},{\bm{I}}_{D})
.
For every
A>0
, there is a constant
C_{\rm off}(A)>0
such that

\Pr[\mathrm{dist}({\bm{X}},m{\mathcal{M}})\geq C_{\rm off}(A)\sigma\sqrt{D\vee\log n}]\leq n^{-A}.

Proof.

In the following, we apply the Gaussian concentration inequality for Lipschitz functions to upper bound
\Pr[\mathrm{dist}({\bm{x}},m{\mathcal{M}})\geq C_{\rm off}(A)\sigma\sqrt{D\vee\log n}]
.
Specifically, let the random point be
{\bm{x}}=m{\bm{y}}+\sigma{\bm{z}}
, where
{\bm{y}}\in{\mathcal{M}}
and
{\bm{z}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}({\bm{0}},{\bm{I}}_{D})
.
We can define a function of the standard Gaussian vector
{\bm{z}}
by:

h({\bm{z}})=\mathrm{dist}(m{\bm{y}}+\sigma{\bm{z}},m{\mathcal{M}}).

Notice that the distance function
\mathrm{dist}(\cdot,{\mathcal{M}})
is
1
-Lipschitz.
Then for any
{\bm{z}}_{1},{\bm{z}}_{2}\sim{\mathcal{N}}({\bm{0}},{\bm{I}}_{D})
, it holds that

\displaystyle|h({\bm{z}}_{1})-h({\bm{z}}_{2})|

\displaystyle=|\mathrm{dist}(m{\bm{y}}+\sigma{\bm{z}}_{1},m{\mathcal{M}})-\mathrm{dist}(m{\bm{y}}+\sigma{\bm{z}}_{2},m{\mathcal{M}})|

\displaystyle\leq\|(m{\bm{y}}+\sigma{\bm{z}}_{1})-(m{\bm{y}}+\sigma{\bm{z}}_{2})\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\|\sigma({\bm{z}}_{1}-{\bm{z}}_{2})\|_{2}=\sigma\|{\bm{z}}_{1}-{\bm{z}}_{2}\|_{2},

which implies that
h({\bm{z}})
is
\sigma
-Lipschitz.
Applying the Gaussian Concentration inequality to the
\sigma
-Lipschitz function
h({\bm{z}})
, we get:

\Pr[h({\bm{z}})-{\mathbb{E}}[h({\bm{z}})]\geq t]\leq\exp\Bigl(-\frac{t^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Bigr).

Since
m{\bm{y}}\in m{\mathcal{M}}
, the distance from
m{\bm{y}}+\sigma{\bm{z}}
to the manifold is upper bounded by the distance to
m{\bm{y}}
itself:

h({\bm{z}})=\mathrm{dist}(m{\bm{y}}+\sigma{\bm{z}},m{\mathcal{M}})\leq\|(m{\bm{y}}+\sigma{\bm{z}})-m{\bm{y}}\|_{2}=\sigma\|{\bm{z}}\|_{2}.

Using the standard bound for the expected norm of a Gaussian vector,
{\mathbb{E}}[\|{\bm{z}}\|_{2}]\leq\sqrt{D}
, we obtain:

{\mathbb{E}}[h({\bm{z}})]\leq\sigma\sqrt{D}.

Set
r=C_{\rm off}(A)\sigma\sqrt{D\vee\log n}
.
Taking
C_{\rm off}(A)
sufficiently large depending only on
A
, we have
t:=r-{\mathbb{E}}[h({\bm{z}})]\geq c_{\rm off}(A)\sigma\sqrt{D\vee\log n}
for a constant
c_{\rm off}(A)
with
c_{\rm off}(A)^{2}/2\geq A
.
Substituting this into the concentration bound, we obtain:

\Pr[h({\bm{z}})\geq C_{\rm off}(A)\sigma\sqrt{D\vee\log n}]\leq\exp\Bigl(-\frac{c_{\rm off}(A)^{2}(D\vee\log n)}{2}\Bigr)\leq n^{-A}.

∎

Lemma C.2

(Off-manifold Fisher information bound)
.

Let
{\bm{Y}}\sim P
be supported on a
d
-dimensional manifold
{\mathcal{M}}\subseteq[0,1]^{D}
.
Fix
m>0
and
\sigma>0
, and define
{\bm{X}}=m{\bm{Y}}+\sigma{\bm{Z}}
, where
{\bm{Z}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}({\bm{0}},{\bm{I}}_{D})
.
Let
p_{\sigma}:{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be the density of
{\bm{X}}
.
For every desired polynomial tail exponent
A>0
, there is a threshold constant
C_{\rm off}(A)>0
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{dist}({\bm{x}},m{\mathcal{M}})\geq C_{\rm off}(A)\sigma\sqrt{D\vee\log n}}\Big\|\frac{\nabla p_{\sigma}({\bm{x}})}{p_{\sigma}({\bm{x}})}\Big\|_{2}^{2}p_{\sigma}({\bm{x}}){\mathrm{d}}{\bm{x}}\lesssim\frac{D}{\sigma^{2}}n^{-A}.

Proof.

Fix
A>0
and let
C_{\rm off}(A)
be chosen at the end of the proof.
By
{\bm{X}}=m{\bm{Y}}+\sigma{\bm{Z}}
, we can apply Tweedie’s formula to express the true score:

\frac{\nabla p_{\sigma}({\bm{x}})}{p_{\sigma}({\bm{x}})}=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}[m{\bm{Y}}-{\bm{X}}|{\bm{X}}={\bm{x}}]=-\frac{1}{\sigma}{\mathbb{E}}[{\bm{Z}}|{\bm{X}}={\bm{x}}].

Consequently, the squared norm evaluates to:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{dist}({\bm{x}},m{\mathcal{M}})\geq C_{\rm off}(A)\sigma\sqrt{D\vee\log n}}\Bigl\|\frac{\nabla p_{\sigma}({\bm{x}})}{p_{\sigma}({\bm{x}})}\Bigr\|_{2}^{2}p_{\sigma}({\bm{x}}){\mathrm{d}}{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{dist}({\bm{x}},m{\mathcal{M}})\geq C_{\rm off}(A)\sigma\sqrt{D\vee\log n}}\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\bigl\|{\mathbb{E}}[{\bm{Z}}|{\bm{X}}={\bm{x}}]\bigr\|_{2}^{2}p_{\sigma}({\bm{x}}){\mathrm{d}}{\bm{x}}

(by Tweedie’s formula)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\|{\mathbb{E}}[{\bm{Z}}|{\bm{X}}]\|_{2}^{2}\ind\{\mathrm{dist}({\bm{X}},m{\mathcal{M}})\geq C_{\rm off}(A)\sigma\sqrt{D\vee\log n}\}\Bigr]

(by definition of expectation)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[{\mathbb{E}}[\|{\bm{Z}}\|_{2}^{2}|{\bm{X}}]\ind\{\mathrm{dist}({\bm{X}},m{\mathcal{M}})\geq C_{\rm off}(A)\sigma\sqrt{D\vee\log n}\}\Bigr]

(by Jensen’s inequality)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\sqrt{{\mathbb{E}}[\|{\bm{Z}}\|_{2}^{4}]\Pr[\mathrm{dist}({\bm{X}},m{\mathcal{M}})\geq C_{\rm off}(A)\sigma\sqrt{D\vee\log n}]}

(by Cauchy-Schwarz)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\sqrt{(D^{2}+2D)\Pr[\mathrm{dist}({\bm{X}},m{\mathcal{M}})\geq C_{\rm off}(A)\sigma\sqrt{D\vee\log n}]}

(by
{\mathbb{E}}[\|{\bm{Z}}\|_{2}^{4}]=D^{2}+2D
)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{D}{\sigma^{2}}\sqrt{\Pr[\mathrm{dist}({\bm{X}},m{\mathcal{M}})\geq C_{\rm off}(A)\sigma\sqrt{D\vee\log n}]}.

(10)

Given the exponent
A>0
in the statement, choose the threshold
C_{\rm off}(A)
large enough so that
Lemma

C.1
applies with exponent
2A
.
Then

\Pr[\mathrm{dist}({\bm{X}},m{\mathcal{M}})\geq C_{\rm off}(A)\sigma\sqrt{D\vee\log n}]\leq n^{-2A}.

Combining this with
Equation

10
gives

\int_{\mathrm{dist}({\bm{x}},m{\mathcal{M}})\geq C_{\rm off}(A)\sigma\sqrt{D\vee\log n}}\Bigl\|\frac{\nabla p_{\sigma}({\bm{x}})}{p_{\sigma}({\bm{x}})}\Bigr\|_{2}^{2}p_{\sigma}({\bm{x}}){\mathrm{d}}{\bm{x}}\lesssim\frac{D}{\sigma^{2}}n^{-A}.

∎

Lemma C.3

(Off-manifold clipped-score error bound)
.

Suppose
1
holds.
Fix
\ell_{\rm off}\geq 1
.
For every
A>0
, let
\varrho_{t}=C_{\varrho}(A)\sigma_{t}\sqrt{D\vee\log n}
, with

C_{\varrho}(A)
chosen large enough for polynomial tail exponent
A
.
For any measurable function
\widetilde{s}:{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\times[{\underline{t}},{\overline{t}}]\to{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
satisfying

\sup_{{\bm{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}\|\widetilde{s}({\bm{x}},t)\|_{\infty}\lesssim\sigma_{t}^{-1}\sqrt{D\ell_{\rm off}},

it holds that
